C5.1 · R0 · 19 OCTOBER 2026

Railway brake research and compact coach-brake feasibility

Railway-brake research and measured space feasibility. The C5 outboard drum/stub proposal is superseded as the development direction. No replacement commercial brake, complete mount or full-motion acceptance is established.

Open dimensioned comparison, sources and space-study CAD.

Development decision

Study bogie-contained tread blocks first, with an inboard axle-disc alternative. This is an engineering inference from railway mechanisms and the current truck geometry. The wheel material/profile, friction pair, actual component envelope, mounting reactions and suspension tracking remain unresolved. The retrieved Wabtec BFC/BFCF sheet lists 860–1,250 mm wheels; it does not establish fit on this 600 mm wheel. Knorr TreadAct documentation establishes the mechanism but supplies no verified mounting general arrangement for this installation.

The old axle-riding shoe carrier uses one torque link per brake. The rotating drum transfers friction torque into the stationary shoe/spider carrier, then through its arm, link, pins and frame bracket. Carrier bearings locate it but do not form a second torque restraint. A compact railway arrangement still needs an explicit reaction path, retention and travel compatibility; placing a brake near a wheel does not establish those.

C5.1 brake architecture: independent force and heat comparison

This issue calculates working demand for the current 13,200 kg regular and 11,000 kg accessible upper coach allocations, four axles per coach and nominal 600 mm wheels. It does not select a brake, establish component strength or approve operation. The 5% rotational-inertia allowance is assumed; actual wheelset and brake inertia remain unknown. No superseded empty-coach mass is used.

Level-track demand (zero grade):

CoachDecelerationTorque per axleTorque per wheelTread tangential force per wheel
Regular0.5 m/s²519.750 Nm259.875 Nm866.25 N
Regular0.7 m/s²727.650 Nm363.825 Nm1212.75 N
Accessible0.5 m/s²433.125 Nm216.562 Nm721.88 N
Accessible0.7 m/s²606.375 Nm303.187 Nm1010.62 N

For the regular coach at 0.7 m/s², a single tread shoe needs 8.085 / 4.851 / 3.465 kN normal force per wheel for assumed block friction of 0.15 / 0.25 / 0.35. These are lining sensitivities, not selected coefficients. An ideally equalized opposed clasp arrangement uses the same total normal force, divided between its two shoes.

One disc per axle carries the full axle torque. At effective contact radii of 150 / 175 / 200 mm, the regular 0.7 m/s² case requires total tangential pad forces of 4.851 / 4.158 / 3.638 kN. Effective radius is the torque-weighted radius of the actual pad contact, not simply rotor outer radius. Pad-normal force requires a selected pad friction coefficient. Supplier “clamping force” conventions must be checked because some describe a per-face force and others the summed force.

At 7.5 mph on level track (zero grade), full-stop heat input is:

CoachWhole coachPer axle / one disc per axlePer wheel / two equally sharing discs per axle
Regular77.902 kJ19.475 kJ9.738 kJ
Accessible64.918 kJ16.230 kJ8.115 kJ

Heat input does not establish temperatures, fade or cooling. Tread braking puts heat into the wheel and requires compatible wheel material, tread profile and friction blocks. Disc braking transfers the friction duty to a rotor but still requires a qualified rotor, hub and caliper installation.

A single shoe at the front or rear point on the horizontal wheel diameter applies a vertical friction force as well as its horizontal normal force. At 0.7 m/s², the regular-coach vertical component is ±1.213 kN per wheel, approximately 7.50% of the ideal static wheel load; the accessible-coach component is ±1.011 kN at the same percentage. Travel direction changes the sign. These are isolated force components, not predicted wheel unloading. An opposed, equally loaded clasp can cancel the force resultants while adding braking torque; real friction, wear and rigging imbalance must be considered. At other shoe angles, the normal force also has a vertical component.

Suspension tracking is separate from wear adjustment. A slack adjuster manages stroke consumed by wear. It does not establish shoe or caliper alignment through the current +35 mm jounce, −40 mm regular / −30 mm accessible rebound, axle tilt and lateral movement. A tread arrangement needs defined hangers or a suitable flexible brake unit. A disc arrangement needs a defined suspended/floating or axle-following caliper support. A drop-prevention catcher is not automatically a second functional brake or independent torque path.

Maximum actuator output sets the mounting load. No actuator, lever ratio, maximum pressure or spring-parking force curve is selected here. Once selected, the maximum credible output—including pressure faults, stroke, tolerances, reverse operation, unequal/seized shoes and permitted service/parking superposition—must determine the beam, hanger, pin, bracket, hub and axle loads. The working torques in this table must not be used as those component load limits. A service-only air chamber does not satisfy the separate spring-parking requirement.

The companion JSON contains four working cases, twelve tread-friction cases, twelve effective-radius disc cases, full formulas, source hashes and 36 arithmetic consistency checks. These checks verify arithmetic only. Current C5 mass and wheel inputs are taken from dist/assets/coach-truck-c5/design-record.json; the retained speed/deceleration comparison is documented in dist/assets/brake-selection-l2/calculations/brake_selection_inputs_R0.json.

Manufacturer context: Knorr-Bremse TreadAct Compact and Wabtec bogie brake equipment. These establish available railway architectures, not fit or acceptance for this coach.

A documented actuator example is ZF WABCO 4231040170 Type 16, a service-only chamber. Its drawing gives 8 bar normal pressure, 10 bar temporarily and at least 57 mm stroke. Force-curve reference labels are 6,080 N at 6 bar and 8,150 N at 8 bar; these are not constant forces or maximum ratings. The ±5% tolerance applies only over 10–40 mm stroke, and no 10 bar force curve was retrieved.

Using the stated 136 mm chamber inner diameter gives a deliberately conservative static pressure-area screening value of14.527 kN at10 bar, ignoring return-spring force and friction. This is an analytical assumption, not a manufacturer output rating; the effective-area bound needs confirmation. Impact, inertia, stops and installation loads remain additional. The example supplies no spring-parking function and is not selected for this vehicle.

C5 · R0 · 19 OCTOBER 2026

Sprung passenger-coach trucks

Drawings, actual parts and CAD states

C5 uses distinct body, sprung and unsprung mass ledgers. Body-bearing loads subtract whole trucks; primary spring loads subtract unsprung mass. The old coach tare numbers have not been reconciled to the heavier complete truck and current seat foundations.

ScreenInterpretation
Spring travel35 mm jounce, 40/30 mm working rebound and 60 mm captured dropout are different conditions. No 2g capacity follows from spring travel alone.
Spring bucklingScherdel seat-factor calculation is conditional on both ends being laterally guided; the unfavorable end condition fails earlier. Material fatigue is separate.
Body contactCompression bearing cannot carry a negative reaction. Cases showing uplift invalidate the maintained-contact solution and require a different contact state or revised envelope.
Roll support1,000 N/mm is a target, with zero nominal gap and shim sensitivity. Actual nonlinear pad curve, friction, preload and available stroke remain open.

Numerical results and governing probes

C5 has a concrete custom coach-truck development layout and reproducible mechanical screens. The current mass allowance is 2,000 kg complete / 1,400 kg unsprung per truck. The earlier 1,800/1,200 comparison is explicitly superseded. Final CAD audit and mass reconciliation are recorded separately; no completed suspension, axle fatigue, brake fit or operating envelope is claimed.

Coil itemRegular coachAccessible coach

Each truck uses eight proposed coils: 18 mm wire, 100 mm mean diameter, seven active/nine total turns, and 148.0911 N/mm calculated rate with G=79 GPa. Nominal solid height is 162 mm; a 5% allowance makes 170.1 mm, leaving 44.9 mm at the 215 mm bump height. Scherdel's ideal v=1 buckling screen assumes lateral end restraint; the v=2 unrestrained cases fail before upper static load. Actual material, rate/height tolerances, guidance, corrosion, preset process and fatigue remain unselected. Spring engagement alone does not establish valid center-bearing contact during rebound.

At 35 mm bump, spring loads reach 1.813/2.031 times upper static load. Regular springs therefore cannot carry a 2× force at that travel without stop reaction; accessible springs reach that ideal force just before the stop. Neither result validates stop energy or dynamic loads. Captured droop is a retention configuration, not a spring-engaged boarding state.

The represented nonbody truck is 1662.01 kg. Adding the explicit 200 kg omitted-OEM-brake allowance gives 1862.01 kg, leaving 137.99 kg within the 2,000 kg development allowance. Moving components plus half the link mass and the same brake allowance total 1291.71 kg; coil participation and other incomplete items remain to reconcile. Body-attached hardware is 62.41 kg per truck and must be counted once in the separate body budget. Its inclusion in the old tare budget remains unresolved. Using 100 kg per OEM brake instead of 50 kg would exceed the complete-truck allowance by 62.01 kg.

Subtract complete truck mass for body-support force and only unsprung mass for primary-coil force. The old coach tare allocations remain unreconciled. Parallel updated tare adds 1,000 kg for two truck allowances increasing from 1,500 to 2,000 kg; accessible adds another 50 kg only if its old 130 kg folding pair becomes C31's 180 kg pair. Both folding assemblies remain aboard in every mode.

The proposed normal equal-heave floor ranges are 777.8–852.8 mm regular and 777.8–842.8 mm accessible. They are not allowed boarding-height ranges. At the C4 threshold point X30,Y−52.25 in about pitch reference X153, ±1° roll adds 0.912 in and ±1° pitch adds 2.147 in. The combined probe already exceeds C4's 2.5 in height condition before heave. Station height and a permitted immobilized boarding condition remain open.

The current sidebearer envelope is 100×50×50 mm with a 1,000 N/mm target, zero running gap and ±0.5 mm shim/preload sensitivity. It needs an effective compression modulus of 10 MPa to meet that rate; no supplier material curve is selected. At ±2° about spherical center Z830.7, contact plane Z470.7 and Y500, geometric closure is 17.67 mm. The CAD procurement target is 20 mm stroke. A volume-conservation probe at 20 mm compression expands the block to 129.10×64.55 mm under equal biaxial strain; actual constrained bulging, pressure, fatigue and friction remain to validate.

The coupled model solves a shared rigid-body roll angle, two truck-frame angles, unilateral pads, gravity moment and all eight wheel reactions. CG height 0.5/1/1.5 m above the actual roll center, CG offset 0/100 mm, lateral acceleration 0/0.4 m/s² and rail crosslevels are sensitivity inputs, not accepted conditions.

The flat legacy-regular local restoring slope is 293.0 kN·m/rad. Separate ideal lower/upper stiffness inequalities are recorded in the JSON, but they do not define a common accepted operating window: combined CG, acceleration, twist and tolerance cases still fail. Negative forces are not clamped. Maximum axle-to-box bearing angle in the combined sensitivity sweep is 3.030°, above the catalog’s conditional 2° guideline; those combined cases are outside the proposed geometry/support range. Actual nonlinear pads, friction, body torsion, damping and stop/catcher redistribution are omitted.

At twice the 45.111 kN equal body-center load, the 200×160×12 fabricated crossbeam gives 54.08 MPa over 1,076 mm clear span, or 62.32 MPa over the conservative 1,240 mm spring-center span. Two 12 mm sideframe webs with 200 mm credited gross depth give 84.58 MPa. These are uniform simply supported gross-section probes; local openings, drop shape, pad-base recesses, load introduction, plate buckling, welds and fatigue are excluded. A ±500 mm body CG shifts the upper regular center maximum to 53.333 kN; the corresponding 2×/3× attachment probes are 106.666/159.999 kN. Lift-retention probe is 39.227 kN per truck, not an operational uplift limit.

Outboard brake relocation avoids a known local girder conflict but lengthens the axle load path. For a 200 mm torque arm, 500 mm link-force overhang and 100 kg brake mass at 560 mm, an 80 mm shaft's nominal combined stress is 22.0/53.8/105.8/316.0 MPa at torque probes 0.364/1/2/6 kNm; a 90 mm stem gives 15.5/37.8/74.3/221.9 MPa. This omits wheel-load bending, bearing-seat transitions, threads, fits, shocks and rotating fatigue. Actual maximum D1 torque and the hub torque joint remain open. The 500 mm link closure calculation is planar; spherical eyes still need a full axle-tilt check.

SKF 22218 E is 90×160×40 mm with shaft abutment at least 101 mm, housing opening at most 149 mm and fillet at most 2 mm. Proposed outboard 22216 E is 80×140×33 with corresponding 91/129/2 mm limits. A removable 96 mm thrust spacer permits the main bearing to install over the 90 mm maximum stem. Fits, bearing clearance, seals, load distribution and life remain open. The SKF catalog ratings are not permitted vehicle loads. General minimum equivalent load for each 22216 E is 2.7 kN; the low-speed oil-lubricated formula gives 0.81 kN. Coasting carrier loads may be lower, so minimum-load/skidding and lubricant suitability remain application holds.

At ±7° yaw and 5,486.4 mm pivot spacing, ideal centerline geometry needs radius at least 22.51 m/73.85 ft. The earlier 50 ft curve needs about 10.37° and redesign. Actual route, wheel profile, back-to-back, contact spacing, wear and flange behavior remain unselected. The 480 mm wheel-contact halfspan used in force calculations is a proxy, not half the 914.4 mm gauge.

Primary sources: SKF printed pages 24–27, Scherdel page 7, and Lee Spring design considerations. Retained D1 Dexter parts establish a brake family, not actual mating dimensions, whole-package clearance or maximum torque. Precise external interfaces do not prevent continued custom development, but no fabrication or operation release is asserted.

C3 R0 · ACCESSIBLE COACH · 19 OCTOBER 2026

Folding-seat layout and mechanism

Open the four drawings, three motion states, full-coach model and fabrication sources. Each wheelchair bay gains one forward-facing folding place. All proposed modes carry 13 passengers, excluding crew: eleven fixed seats plus two seated passengers, one seated passenger and one wheelchair rider, or two wheelchair riders. This issue is a preliminary physical design and calculation record; the complete accessible coach and construction release remain unfinished.

Layout and accessibility basis

Two six-inch permanent strips at X60–66 and X126–132 hold the folded mechanisms. Clear wheelchair bays are X66–126 and X132–192, Y−52 to −16. A 36-inch route remains at Y−12 to +24. Companion cushions start at X86 and X152; rear fixed rows start at X194, X226 and X258. Floor is Z32 above rail, subject to unresolved vehicle suspension/load state. Each full stowed physical assembly occupies nominal X60–65.875 and Y−47 to −17, then repeats +66 inches: only ⅛ inch remains before the wheelchair boundary and no aft strip allowance remains at the foot. Final tolerance, coating, welds and retention hardware must be reconciled before release.

US Access Board amusement-ride provisions are a preliminary reference, pending classification/applicability review. They address clear wheelchair space, entry maneuvering, adjoining routes and companion seating. The 42-inch entry and 36 × 60-inch maneuvering reservation follow an advisory example; they are not universal proof of access. Door hardware, threshold/boarding gap, platform slope, securement and evacuation remain separate unfinished interfaces. No ADA or rail-vehicle compliance is asserted.

Wheelchair maneuvering evidence

The checked rigid proxy is 30 × 48 inches, with its rear axle 12 inches from the rear. It advances/reverses along its heading and turns about the rear axle; there is no sideways sliding. The normal entry is provisionally the negative-Y 42-inch opening. Obstacles include fixed-seat envelopes, 9½-inch forward leg reservations, complete stow strips and an occupied other bay. An analytic reverse arc replaces the first bay’s inefficient discrete-search shunts. Each final path has one travel-direction reversal. A separate polygon-distance verifier checks the saved paths and bounds movement between samples.

Proxy pathMinimum sampled clearanceConservative between-sample lower boundCollisions
Bay 11.179 in1.054 in0
Bay 21.024 in0.899 in0

Both computed parked positions lie inside their assigned bays. The other bay was checked as an occupied wheelchair proxy and as an occupied folding-seat proxy. Time reversal gives the same geometric exit envelope; it does not establish practical assisted or independent operation. Companion shoulder alignment is illustrative and must be verified with representative users/devices. The old accessible body is used only as context, raised four inches to the current floor datum. The regular-coach C1 centered 32-inch end doorway and side posts conflict with this accessible layout and must not be reused unchanged.

Hinge, stops and positive locking

Each pan rotates 90 degrees about Y at X64/Z48.5 (second X130). The 24-inch-wide pan has rear corner relief for the fixed posts. Two ½-inch sectors connect through 2-inch-deep tangs to 1½-inch-square pan rails. Each ¾-inch hinge pin passes through a nominal bronze bushing OD1/ID0.753 inches in the sector and two ⅜-inch fixed cheeks. Two ⅝-inch positive locks engage separate sector holes on a 1.15-inch radius in either end position. Hard stops are separate from the locks; the deployed land lies at local X+1 to +1.5, nominal effective arm 1.25 inches.

The two lock holes are at 45°/135°; the fixed index is local X−0.813173/Z+0.813173. Mid-travel pins are withdrawn. Positive secondary retainers, captive guides, indication, guard fasteners, the rear-gap shroud and safe handholds remain incomplete. There is no claimed spring assist or automatic locking. Proposed prototype conversion uses two crew in an empty, stationary coach, with continuous support of the pan and verification of both fully engaged pins before boarding.

Mechanism load comparisons

The separate 450-lbf front-edge comparison has an 18-inch lever. Equal load sharing gives 3,240 lbf per stop and 3,015 lbf per hinge; a full one-sided case gives 6,480 and 6,030 lbf. The nominal ¾-inch pin combined bending/shear screen is 38.3 ksi in that one-sided case. Moving the assumed stop resultant to the near edge increases this to 48.6 ksi. Contact arm and contact area are coupled: a full-land mean pressure does not bound actual edge pressure or indentation. A specified alloy pin with a proposed 90-ksi minimum yield target is a procurement/design criterion, not a selected or certified part.

The 2-inch tang reduces the one-sided gross bending comparison to 22.2 ksi; local transitions, holes, welds, fatigue and pan load spreading remain unresolved. A conservative captured-load/misuse case of 165 kg at 1.5g upward through a 9-inch lever drives the locking-pin comparison. A ⅝-inch lock gives about 46.1 ksi versus 89.0 ksi for the rejected ½-inch concept. An unrestrained rider does not normally pull the pan upward after losing contact; this is an explicit assumed misuse envelope, not a predicted occupant load or restraint design. The actual operating and acceptance loads still need agreement.

Two-foot foundation and rail-end demand

C3 has its own cantilever foundation. Foot centers are X62.5 (second X128.5), Y−44/−20; post centers are X61.25 (second X127.25). Each foot has a 5 × 6 × ¾-inch cap, a 5 × 6 × 1-inch sole, four ½-inch bolts on a 3½ × 4-inch pattern, a closed ⅜-inch rail saddle and ½-inch ribs. The sole underside is Z32.25, giving nominal ¼-inch clearance above the floor. Matched steel spacers are 12.6 mm nominal. The 3¼-inch bolt envelope is provisional; real nut/washer/grip, locking, preload, tools and flexible floor closeouts remain to select.

The foundation screen uses a 145-kg occupant and 65-kg assembly allowance. Analyst cases include ±1g longitudinal, ±0.5g lateral and zero/1.5g downward, plus separate 300-lbf back and 450-lbf front comparisons. Occupant/front-patch transverse offsets of ±9½ inches are included; combined occupant/assembly inertia has the corresponding mass-weighted offset. Equal and offset-dependent pitch sharing and three roll distributions are compared. These are discrete stiffness assumptions, not a coupled flexible contact solution.

Independent review exposed the missing off-center loading in the first screen; adding it increased bolt demand from 8.44 to 13.05 kN and drove the one-inch sole. A 2× force amplification gives about 223 MPa in the cap strip and 219 MPa in the sole strip. The assumed effective strip width and amplification are sensitivity parameters, not a demonstrated bound on prying or an accepted joint capacity. Ribs, welds, local walls, slip, contact and fatigue remain unresolved.

The existing longitudinal rails must carry these demands into their end crossmembers. A possible external faceplate/internal-diaphragm revision is described separately, but it has not been modeled, assigned mass or credited with stiffness. Gross rail results assume stated end restraint; they do not establish the current end joint or thin crossmember wall can provide it. See the foundation note and complete concurrent load records.

Mass, handling and verification

Nominal metal, mounts and main hardware total 54.06 kg per folding seat, or 108.11 kg for both. The complete-seat trial allowance remains 65 kg each/130 kg for the pair; unmodeled reinforcement consumes that allowance. The moving CAD assembly including placeholder padding is 13.15 kg. At an 18-inch hand radius, calculated gravity-only force is about 12.1 lbf; the conservative 20-kg moving allowance gives 22.0 lbf. Friction, binding, grip and real crew effort are not included. No complete accessible-coach tare is asserted.

All 98 reported nominal solids are valid. Exact solid intersections show no unintended penetration in the declared static pairs, at 19 angles from 0° to 90° in 5° increments, or in the checked inserted/withdrawn pin states. This is a sampled nominal check, not continuous swept-volume or tolerance proof. Actual guards, welds, upholstery and retention may change the envelope. Calculation checks cover stated equilibrium/scaling identities; foundation force/moment balances were checked across 198 foot cases. No physical test, durability result or professional acceptance is claimed. Browser automation was unavailable; data, links and JavaScript syntax were checked and CAD drawings/renders were visually reviewed.

Braced bench and compact ribbed feet

Selected geometry and weight

The A36 candidate cap is 3½ ×6 ×⅝ inches; the sole is 3½ ×6 ×½ inches. Upper/lower ribs are⅜ inch thick and retain 1¾ inch depth through the bolt line, with only the outboard tail beveled. The retained four-bolt pattern is local X±⅞/Y±2 inches. Sole underside staysZ32.25 above nominal floorZ32; rigid spacer length becomes 15.775 mm. The complete-seat budget remains 65 kg per bench /1,040 kg per regular coach. Frame comparison

The reproducible 3D elastic beam model uses catalog A/I/S/J/C and section masses, six degrees of freedom per node, E200 GPa and independent support springs. Main frame intersections remain rigid. Brace-end rotations vary from released to rigid; foot rotational stiffness varies 0–10 MN·m/rad, with additional axial and mixed-foot sensitivities. These are analyst ranges, not measured or calibrated joints. Panels are not used as structural diaphragms.

All variants retain 355 kg comparison mass: two 145 kg occupants plus 65 kg bench allowance. Local mount: retain the lower reference level

Forces are mapped to CAD feet by coordinates: F1(−8,20), F2(−8,44), F3(8,20), F4(8,44). Solver numbering alone would swap F2/F3. Loads are transferred separately to the sole underside, cap top and cap underside. Moving the wrench downward byh adds−hFy toMx and+hFx toMy. Reusing sole moments at the lower cap would omit consequential bending from horizontal load.

The declared strip screen uses bolt-to-rib-face span 0.6875 in and net strip width 1.1875 in after the 9/16-inch hole. An equivalent corner allocation uses T₀=max(N,0)/4+|My|/(4 a)+|Mx|/(4 b), with a=0.875 in,b=2 in. Absolute contact demand uses |N| instead. The cap allocation is an equivalent wrench representation; it is not proof that actual bolt tension grows by the same amount. Bolt bending, shear and bearing also participate across the spacer gap.

Geometry checks and practical limits

The final CAD has 155 prototype mesh parts. All reported solids are valid and the listed non-hardware interference check reports 0 positive-volume intersections. The rear V braces use a common miter atY32 to remove an apex collision found during checking. Braces contact the nominal receiving faces, but this does not define weld throat, root gap, actual stock fit or capacity.

The 1.25-inch-diameter long socket approach meets the rear V at four bolt locations. Short 2-inch and lower approach cylinders clear the bare modeled parts. Bare-rib radial clearance is only 1/16 inch after thickening the ribs; weld fillets can intrude. Finished-joint tool access is therefore unverified. Floor isolation is nominal; a flexible closure must not create a structural floor load path. Upholstery, locking, local panel fasteners and occupied leg/aisle clearance still need development.

Numerical verification covers independent axial/bending/torsion cantilevers, rotated beams, rigid-body patch response, the released-brace truss limit, sign reversal and moment reference changes. Across 2,520 frame cases, maximum force/moment equilibrium residual was 1.45×10⁻⁶N orN·m. This checks implementation, not physical validity. 3O.2 · C2 R0 · 19 OCTOBER 2026

Bench and floor-mount development

Open the drawings, parts and rotatable assembly. C2 replaces the seat-space boxes with custom parts. The regular coach retains 32 places. The whole coach and fabrication release remain incomplete.

Decisions and calculations

The inherited two-bolt line could not establish local fore/aft moment resistance, and the sole had zero floor clearance. C2 uses four bolts, a raised steel bypass and revised panel openings. The concurrent longitudinal/lateral sensitivity made the earlier ⅝-inch gross cap strip approximately 247 MPa before prying. Both cap and sole are now ¾-inch preliminary candidates; the corresponding gross cap-strip result is approximately 171.8 MPa. This excludes real contact, effective width, two-way action, prying, holes, welds and fatigue; it is not a passing joint design.

Two 145 kg occupants plus a 65 kg complete-seat allowance give 355 kg for the analyst inertia screen. The nominal metal assembly is 61.5 kg per bench, including hardpoints and nominal main fasteners; it excludes finishes, pads and small hardware. The old 15 kg seat allowance is rejected. The new trial regular-coach seating allowance is 1,040 kg. This must be reconciled into vehicle mass/CG and propulsion/brake calculations; it is not a measured coach tare.

APTA passenger-seat static tests are used only as comparison load patterns: both directions on the back and localized front-edge loading. They have not been established as the governing attraction standard. The backrest local fixed-foot bound requires approximately 13.25 kN per tension-row bolt before prying. The concurrent X/Y inertia sensitivity is higher: approximately 18.18 kN at the most-loaded corner. These are separate stiffness bounds, not a proved force distribution. Catalogue proof properties do not establish joint capacity.

Nominal floor separation is 6.35 mm; actual stack/thermal/loaded movement must retain the accepted gap. The proposed 65 kg complete-seat allowance is provisional, and mass reduction remains a design objective. The present metal leaves only a small remainder for pads, finishes and small hardware; next compare a lighter braced frame and realistic foot stiffness rather than repeatedly thickening plates under an assumed fixed-foot load path.

3O.1 · COMPLETED AUDIT ISSUE · 19 OCTOBER 2026

Preserve useful space. Correct the actual floor connection.

This is a completed source/connection audit, not completed Phase3O or new fabrication drawings. The saved geometry reveals a real zero-clearance seat/floor conflict. No unsupported mount dimensions have been added.

The saved seat soleplate can contact the floor

The 4.25 × 6.50 × 0.25 in soleplate overlaps its 2.75 × 6.25 in floor opening by 0.75 in per x end and 0.125 in per y side. Its underside and the finished floor both lie at z=32.000 in: nominal vertical isolation is zero. This is plane contact, not a positive-volume CAD clash. Tolerances, bow and loaded deflection can put seat load into the panel or clamp its thermal movement. The previous “floor bypass” is therefore not a completed isolated connection.

The section below uses the actual preserved I1 plates, bores, hollow spacers and rail envelope, not a substitute brake-style diagram. It exposes the problem; it does not approve the inherited mount.

Open full-size coordinate-derived section ↗ · Retained rotatable I1 connection study

What the current interior actually contains

The retained clear study cabin is306 ×104in (25ft6in ×8ft8in), not a30ft-long seating compartment. Regular:32guests, sixteen40in two-adult benches,32in pitch,20in seat envelope depth,24in central aisle and31in end clear zones. The12in gap between consecutive seat envelopes is not a verified knee/coat or evacuation clearance.

Accessible:11fixed seats plus2reserved wheelchair spaces. The36in aisle and36in end doors already share the same+6in offset centerline. There is no door/aisle alignment defect to fix. Source side openings are42in wide at x=9..51in. Hardware, actual maneuvering, securement/companion arrangement and boarding/evacuation still need detailed coordination and an applicable-requirements determination.

These are audited space envelopes, not physical seat/body fabrication geometry. The3in wall, roof envelope and old28in floor models do not define the new load-bearing body. The later32in floor datum has no accepted empty/loaded suspension state. No station threshold may be fixed from it.

Twenty-one panels need an actual movement and retention scheme

Five central panels each contain six frame-referenced seat penetrations. A floor panel is not proven to expand freely about one center just because its seams have gaps. The inherited round-hole clip/insert, finish/adhesive continuity and overlapping soleplates can introduce restraints.

For a single center locator per freely sliding panel, g = g₀(1+εsteel) − (εpanel−εsteel)(Lᵢ+Lⱼ)/2. The full record also covers arbitrary anchor locations and opening-to-cap motion. Multiple rigid anchors require thermal-stress analysis, not just free-expansion arithmetic.

Illustration only:αpanel=23 andαsteel=12µm/m/K, both+60K. These are analyst inputs, not measured composite properties or selected temperature limits. Starting seam3.175mm:

Center-located sensitivityClosure mmNominal remaining mm
equal longitudinal seam0.80032.3747
last longitudinal seam0.59912.5759
central/wing seam0.74162.4334

No tolerance or seal/creep/skew allowance has yet been deducted. A different global single-anchor half-length model gives2.6152mm mismatch at156in, greater than the inherited1.5875mm perimeter inset. These two models describe different restraints; do not combine them or conclude that a particular gap is adequate. All64penetrations are separately evaluated in the editable record under the center-locator probe only.

A structural panel seam is not a vinyl finish-edge gap. A manufacturer’s tensile elongation at break is not a cyclic bond-shear allowance. New seam treatment, inserts, closeouts, fastener preload, coatings, moisture isolation and floor retention must work as one system.

Current and historical evidence are separate. Carraro currently lists TCB80 in an engine-power application band; that is not a rated train mass or converter map. Parker currently lists the GVM/GVI families. The exact GVM winding figures below come from its 2015 catalog and a corroborating later hosted technical manual, not a confirmed current order. Attempts to retrieve the newest linked catalogs returned a redirect fetch error. Availability and exact current configuration remain unconfirmed.

Power comparison with explicit assumed efficiencies

All rows use the same 65.1 t upper allocation, 0% grade, coefficient 0.005, 2 kN extra drag and 7.5 mph. Wheel demand is 17.41 kW. Each adds the same 10 kW auxiliary normalization so the conversion-chain effect is visible. Actual auxiliary demand differs by architecture; these are not installed-package or fuel-consumption predictions. Hotel HVAC power is excluded.

Comparison modelAssumed shaft-to-wheel efficiencyEngine demand including normalized auxiliaries
hydrostatic inherited constant efficiencies69.4%35.07 kW
locked mechanical assumed0.8585.0%30.48 kW
locked mechanical assumed0.9090.0%29.34 kW
locked mechanical assumed0.9595.0%28.32 kW
diesel-electric assumed chain74.8%33.28 kW

Electric example assumptions: generator 0.90 × rectifier 0.98 × inverter 0.97 × motor 0.92 × gears 0.95 = 0.74774. The locked-mechanical probes cannot represent converter slip at launch/crawl. Supplier maps and real duty must replace every assumed efficiency before choosing for fuel use or cooling.

Gearing is specific to the motor and launch arrangement

Electric reference: higher speed requires a different reduction

The manufacturer’s historical GVM210-100-SPW table at 320 VDC gives rated 78.6 N·m / 53.5 kW at 6,500 rpm, peak torque 173 N·m and maximum speed 8,000 rpm. The table assumes 65°C coolant inlet and an optimal inverter association without drive limitation. It does not establish standstill torque, accepted peak duration or compatibility with a particular inverter.

Matching that 6,500 rpm point to 7.5 mph on trial 600 mm wheels gives a mathematical total ratio of 60.905:1, rather than the hydrostatic trial 9:1. With assumed 95% gear efficiency, the rated-torque wheel-force proxy is 15.16 kN. Steady baseline motor demand is 26.92 N·m; the separate 0.1 m/s² breakaway probe needs 77.22 N·m. These comparisons do not prove low-speed cooling, torque availability, transient power or adhesion.

The peak-torque proxy would reach 33.37 kN before adhesion, substantially changing shaft, gearcase, coupling and control load cases. It is not an acceptable uncontrolled rail force, and peak torque cannot be combined with rated speed. The engine/generator chain independently limits output: under the assumed electric efficiencies the 45.4 kW main-engine allocation gives only 33.95 kW at the wheels, or about 10.13 kN at 7.5 mph. It cannot supply the motor’s full 53.5 kW rating. A high-speed motor cannot reuse the hydrostatic gearing or mounting drawing merely because its kW figure is similar.

Mechanical reference: a cruising ratio does not solve launch

At the inherited gross-ceiling allocation, 45.4 kW at 2,600 rpm is a 166.75 N·m mean main-shaft torque proxy. A 24.362:1 total ratio matches 7.5 mph with trial 600 mm wheels. At assumed 95% gear efficiency it gives a 12.86 kN force proxy, below the 14.89 kN separate 0.1 m/s² breakaway probe.

The corresponding low-ratio probe would be 28.207:1. These are algebraic speed/torque comparisons, not selected gears or converter ratios. A rigidly coupled fixed ratio cannot start the train from zero while keeping a diesel running. An actual clutch/converter launch map, shift/reverse controls, low-speed heat and duty are required; a locked-efficiency row cannot supply that evidence.

Diesel-electric adds a complete electrical energy path

A traction motor is not a diesel-electric system by itself. The generator, power conversion, bus, cooling and fault response must be developed together:

  • Generator torsional coupling, net output map and engine controls
  • Rectifier/DC bus regulation, precharge, contactors, fusing, isolation/ground-fault strategy and high-voltage interlocks
  • Matched motor/inverter current, speed, resolver/encoder, thermal and torque limits
  • Battery or braking chopper/resistor energy path for regeneration; no assumption diesel generator absorbs braking energy
  • Separate hotel-power load/supply coordination; no counting the same generator capacity twice
  • Defined safe coast/stop behavior after bus/inverter faults and independent air-brake application

At 65.1 t and exactly 7.5 mph, translational kinetic energy is 365.90 kJ (0.10164 kWh), excluding rotating inertia. This small energy figure does not size a braking resistor or battery: peak power, repeated stops, acceptable bus voltage, heat rejection and fault conditions still govern. No regenerative-braking credit is taken. The separate air service/emergency/spring-parking architecture remains required.

A current GVI075 family listing advertises 75 kW continuous, but that alone does not establish current, voltage, feedback, regeneration or rail suitability. Do not pair it with the reference motor on nameplate power alone.

Exact checkpoint: continue independent coach/body and HVAC coordination

Completed review issues: 3N.1 original-workbook audit, 3N.2 motor/circuit reconciliation, and this 3N.3 alternatives comparison. The custom locomotive drivetrain is not geometrically defined or released. N-G01–N-G07 and the whole-truck/coupling interfaces remain open.

The next independent task is 3O.1: audit the latest J1a coach body/interior and physical interfaces, then coordinate thermal/electrical demand into 3P. Retain maximum practical interior space, the separate accessible variant and the unresolved floor load state. Recover earlier thermal source before reusing loads. This is continuing useful work while exact drivetrain and axlebox/brake interfaces remain unavailable; it is not a change to the train’s overall requirements.

NEW · 3N.2 MOTOR-COUNT / CIRCUIT REVIEW · 19 OCTOBER 2026

Choose the drive arrangement before drawing its mounts.

3N.1 retained one H1B 110 motor with mechanically distributed drive as the inherited candidate. This issue reconciles paired motors against the same pump, engine-power limit and suspension interfaces. It completes a motor-count comparison, not the locomotive installation design or Phase 3N.

Development direction: retain the single-motor/mechanical-distribution route as the lead comparison, and two smaller parallel motors as an alternative. Series H1B motors are not carried as the preferred path without specific OEM acceptance. A final topology still needs actual axle loads, gear geometry, suspension travel, fault/control and supplier review.

Motor-count calculations ↓Unequal flow and added heat ↓How the drive must attach ↓

Same pump: more motors trade speed for torque

All rows use the inherited 111.15 L/min delivered flow, 600 mm wheels and constant efficiency assumptions. Paired rows assume equal flow to each motor. H1B 060 is a real 60 cc family; the 55 cc row is a trial displacement-control target, not a delivered factory stop or exact order code.

ComparisonMotor count / each displacementTrial reductionFull-flow speed300 bar wheel-force proxy
H1B110 single, inherited1 × 110 cc9:17.496 mph13.47 kN
H1B060 pair at full displacement, same gearing2 × 60 cc9:16.871 mph14.70 kN
H1B110 pair, same gearing2 × 110 cc9:13.748 mph26.94 kN

Full flow and 300 bar are not available together from the original engine allowance. Every row would require 65.0 kW at the main pump shaft under these assumptions, before the 10 kW auxiliaries. The inherited gross ceiling leaves only 45.4 kW for that shaft. The table compares geometry/flow trade-offs, not approved operating points.

Two 60 cc motors with the same 9:1 reduction give 6.871 mph, not the inherited 7.4955 mph. Changing the reduction to the trial 8.25:1 rematches speed and brings the total force proxy back to the single-motor result. It does not create more power. The ratios are mathematical comparisons, not chosen gear-tooth counts.

Equations and limitations

With N parallel motors at equal flow, each motor rpm = Qtotal × 1000 × ηmv / (N × Dmotor). Combined wheel torque = N × Δp × Dmotor × ηmt × reduction × ηgear / (2π). The same pump flow/pressure and assumed efficiency give the same output power, while displacement and ratio trade speed against torque.

Real motor leakage, control loss, starting behavior and mechanical losses differ with size and configuration. Shared engine power remains a combined limit. A second pump would also require an accepted split of engine torque, charge supply and controls; it does not duplicate the engine power.

Check one motor taking all flow — not just equal sharing

The all-flow screen uses full delivery to one branch. The first speed column retains both 95% volumetric-efficiency assumptions; the second removes their leakage credit at the same 45 cc / 2,600 rpm pump point. It is not an overall upper bound: actual engine no-load/overspeed and overrun must also be checked.

Motor / displacement endpointInherited-efficiency flow proxyNo-leakage screen at same pump pointOEM rated / maximum speed at endpoint
H1B060 at 60 cc1760 rpm1950 rpm3600 / 4500 rpm
H1B060 at 12 cc8799 rpm9750 rpm5900 / 7250 rpm
H1B110 at 110 cc960 rpm1064 rpm2900 / 3700 rpm
H1B110 at 22 cc4800 rpm5318 rpm4800 / 5950 rpm

A 060 motor unexpectedly at its normal 12 cc minimum with all pump flow exceeds the listed maximum speed in both screens. Even the 110 at 22 cc cannot be declared below its rated speed by crediting the inherited efficiencies. Exact displacement stops, control fail state, speed sensing, reverse and retarding behavior need an accepted protection design.

Divider loss must enter both the power and oil-heat budgets

These rounded pressure-drop probes are near the broad range discussed in the OEM circuit guide, not a selected valve. At the inherited 7.4955 mph and 111.15 L/min, baseline engine-plus-auxiliary demand is 35.05 kW and main-loop oil heat is 6.74 kW. The following adds a divider pressure drop while holding motor work and flow unchanged:

Valve drop probeValve heat aloneAdditional pump shaft demandTotal engine + auxiliariesTotal drive/valve oil heat
13.8 bar2.56 kW2.99 kW38.04 kW9.73 kW
27.6 bar5.11 kW5.98 kW41.03 kW12.72 kW
41.4 bar7.67 kW8.97 kW44.02 kW15.71 kW

Valve heat = Δpvalve × Qdelivered / 600 in kW. Extra pump input = valve heat / (ηpv ηpm). Total oil heat is pump shaft input minus motor mechanical output. The total already includes valve heat and the additional pump loss; do not add valve heat twice. Gear heat, charge/control/flush losses and engine cooling remain separate.

The case above is a matched aggregate 110 cc motor-displacement comparison. It does not claim the actual loss curve of a 60 cc pair or a particular flow divider.

Primary circuit evidence and decision

Danfoss warns that series axial/bent-axis motor arrangements can reduce bearing life and calls for application review. Its parallel-circuit guidance identifies unequal-load overspeed, divider heat and reverse/combining pressure effects. These findings keep series motors out of the preferred route and prevent treating a generic divider as an accepted traction control.

Any advancing parallel design must resolve bidirectional flow control, actual flow range, flushing/charge balance, motor inlet/case pressure and retarding faults with controlled valve data and tests. One-way splitting is not a reversing-train solution. No valve or pressure setting is selected here.

Physical connections: which structure moves with which

This is a connection-definition register, not a drawing of completed fabricated parts. Locomotive topology is still unresolved. It must coordinate the entire powered running gear before a motor plate, gearcase, torque rod or axle connection is dimensioned.

A single motor at the 300 bar trial has a 472.69 N·m shaft-torque proxy and 4,041.50 N·m combined wheel torque. Two driven axles do not justify sizing each branch for exactly half: the accepted distribution and traction-fault cases may make one branch govern. Actual input/output axes, housing forces and torque reaction need vector equilibrium, reversal/transient and fatigue checks.

Voith’s real rail-drive descriptions distinguish sprung drives, axle-riding gearcases, movement-compensating couplings/cardan shafts and torque reaction into the frame. They support the need for explicit movement paths, not a purchase selection or a size suitable for our train. Dana’s simple angle calculator does not cover compound horizontal angles. Actual loaded/unloaded/travel geometry, joint phasing, plunge and critical speed remain necessary.

What the present candidate can — and cannot — demonstrate

The current upper mass allocation is 65,100 kg. The target remains 0% longitudinal grade. With the inherited running-resistance coefficient 0.005 and an additional 2 kN drag probe, calculated steady demand is 26.71 kW at 5 mph or 35.07 kW at 7.5 mph, including the assumed 10 kW traction auxiliaries. This does not include coach HVAC/hotel power.

The result does not demonstrate real capacity. Actual resistance, net continuous engine output, charge/fan/compressor duty, adverse adhesion and heat rejection are still unknown. Under these assumptions it is worth continuing this smaller-engine candidate rather than selecting a larger engine solely from train mass. It is not yet a procurement recommendation.

Open calculated envelope ↗. This is a performance chart, not a fabrication drawing. The 35 and 45 kW budgets are installed-power sensitivities, not Kubota continuous ratings.

Travel speedAdditional drag probeTotal rail forceEngine + 10 kW auxiliariesMain-loop oil heat only
5 mph0 kN3.19 kN20.27 kW2.76 kW
7.5 mph0 kN3.19 kN25.41 kW4.15 kW
5 mph2 kN5.19 kN26.71 kW4.49 kW
7.5 mph2 kN5.19 kN35.07 kW6.74 kW
5 mph5 kN8.19 kN36.37 kW7.09 kW
7.5 mph5 kN8.19 kN49.55 kW10.64 kW

All rows: upper mass 65.1 t, coefficient 0.005, constant assumed shaft-to-wheel efficiency 69.45%. Exact 7.5 mph needs slightly more flow than this full-displacement 110 cc / 9:1 / 600 mm combination provides. The untrimmed calculated limit is 7.4955 mph; do not round it into a demonstrated speed.

Launch, adhesion and flow/power boundary

At the 300 bar probe, the constant torque-efficiency model gives 13.47 kN before adhesion. Breakaway resistance with coefficient 0.010 and 2 kN extra is 8.38 kN. Conditional acceleration is 0.0781 m/s² if that torque is available and the rail can transmit it. Neither low-speed torque nor that adhesion has been demonstrated.

With 8 t on driven wheels and trial adhesion 0.10, available rail force is only 7.85 kN: this breakaway case does not start. With 10 t and 0.10 it is 9.81 kN, giving only 0.0219 m/s². The 14.5 t locomotive/hotel allowance must not be assumed to be all driven adhesive mass.

The separate 0.1 m/s² launch probe needs 14.89 kN, corresponding to 331.68 bar with this motor/gearing model. That exceeds the present 300 bar study point. A higher setting is not authorized by being below a catalog pressure limit.

At a 55.4 kW gross ceiling minus 10 kW auxiliaries, the main pump has 45.4 kW. Full 45 cc stroke at 2,600 rpm gives 111.15 L/min under the assumed volumetric efficiency, but only 209.54 bar can be sustained within that power allocation. At 300 bar, power limitation requires about 31.43 cc and limits the same-displacement motor to about 5.235 mph. A pressure rating alone cannot supply maximum flow and pressure together.

Traceable equations and scope

v = mph × 0.44704. Required force F = m g c + additional drag, on 0% grade. Required wheel power = F v. Main-pump shaft power = wheel power / (ηpv ηpm ηmv ηmt ηgear). The engine comparison adds the unallocated 10 kW auxiliaries.

Motor torque = Δp Dmotor ηmt / (2π). Wheel force = motor torque × reduction × ηgear / wheel radius. Delivered pump flow = Dpump × pump rpm × ηpv / 1000 in L/min. Motor rpm = delivered flow × 1000 × ηmv / Dmotor. Pump input power in kW = Δp[bar] × Dpump[cc/rev] × rpm / (600000 ηpm). Use the high-minus-low loop pressure, not high-side gauge pressure alone.

The 162 steady cases, 108 launch/adhesion cases and 450 force-speed samples are sensitivities, not a validated transient simulation. Rotating inertia, hose elasticity/losses, actual maps, wheel-load transfer and measured curve resistance are excluded. Constant efficiencies cannot predict stalled heating, neutral creep or cogging.

Recovered workbook: what was retained and revised

The original Train_Danfoss_Matching_Calculation_R0.xlsx, dated 18 October 2026, was inspected read-only. All 53 formula cells were independently replayed from their original precedents and agree with the stored results. The original file is unchanged. Cell addresses, formulas, values and its SHA-256 are in the source audit.

Retained: 65.1 t upper allocation, candidate pump/motor, 0% grade, and explicitly trial 600 mm wheels/9:1 ratio. Revised interpretation: intermittent output is not continuous output; torque proxy is not verified launch performance; the auxiliary allowance excludes hotel demand; and an exact shaft/control order code is required.

Current OEM checks confirmed the main displacement/speed/pressure entries. The motor URL served a newer document revision 1303 despite its 1302 filename. This is recorded rather than silently treated as the old document.

A family match is not an input-shaft match

The H1P 045 technical information lists different shaft torque capacities. These are OEM catalog comparisons, not selected coupling or shaft ratings for our installation.

H1P input optionCatalog rated / maximum torqueFull 45 cc at 300 bar proxy: 238.7 N·m
G1 · 14-tooth534 / 592 N·mBelow listed figures; interface and torsion still unresolved
G4 · 13-tooth180 / 222 N·mAbove listed maximum — cannot assume this option
G5 · 15-tooth277 / 370 N·mBelow listed figures; not an accepted selection

At the 45.4 kW main-shaft allocation, mean torque is 166.75 N·m at 2,600 rpm. Maintaining that same power at 1,600 rpm would require 270.96 N·m; that is a power sensitivity, not an assertion about the engine’s accepted output there. Full 45 cc at 420 bar would require 334.23 N·m under the assumed mechanical efficiency and may not be engine-feasible.

Final input torque must include any charge/auxiliary loads driven through that shaft, engine torque pulses, reversals and transients. Exact spline engagement, coupling support, alignment, lubrication and bellhousing must be coordinated with the delivered engine and pump. Do not machine an adapter from the family name.

At the motor output, the reduction and axle drive need their own supported load path. Do not put an assumed chain, pulley or unsupported gear on the H1B shaft without the OEM side-load/bearing-life review.

Source N-S06, printed pages 54–56. The minimum specified active spline length and OEM shaft-rating definitions are part of those ratings. No order code is selected.

Oil heat, charge flow and cooling at low train speed

The original main-loop heat result, 12.21 kW, is the gross-ceiling drive-loss estimate, not a cooler selection. The reduction contributes a separate 1.66 kW at that ceiling. Baseline steady main-loop oil heat is 4.49 kW at 5 mph or 6.74 kW at 7.5 mph. Engine coolant/intercooler/exhaust heat is separate and has not been supplied.

For a screening example only, add a 3 kW oil-side loss probe to the 12.21 kW main drive loss. With trial 80°C oil inlet and 46°C cooling-air inlet, the exchanger must reject 15.21 kW at a 34 K inlet difference: 0.447 kW/K. This ratio is not true exchanger UA. The 27 thermal cases expose sensitivity to additional losses and cooling-air temperature; they do not select oil temperature or Southern Nevada design weather.

Actual oil flow, viscosity, pressure drop, fan/airflow, hot-air recirculation, fouling and altitude must be matched to installed cooler curves. If the oil cooler receives air already warmed by the radiator, use that warmer inlet air. Provide engineered airflow at near-zero vehicle speed. A decorative steam-era shell cannot be assumed to ventilate the heat exchangers.

The constant volumetric efficiencies imply leakage-only charge proxies of 7.61 L/min at 5 mph and 11.41 L/min at the untrimmed maximum speed. These are not charge-pump capacities. Add actual flushing, control and other losses; verify cold/hot low-loop, control, inlet and drain pressure over all engine speeds. Keep case drains free of unacceptable cooler/filter backpressure.

The pump manual distinguishes 500 rpm minimum rotation from 1,175 rpm minimum for full performance with internal charge. Idle traction capability must be reviewed, not assumed. The component temperature and case-pressure limits must be reconciled with exact options before choosing alarms, oil, reservoir or plumbing.

Traction controls must coordinate with independent air brakes

The candidate hydrostatic transmission is not the independent stopping or parking system. Retain the 32 coach Type 20/24 chambers; the locomotive brake package remains separate and unresolved. The earlier air-volume entries are catalog references whose free-air interpretation still requires confirmation. They cannot justify compressor power or reservoir sizing by themselves.

The brake design must stop and hold after hydrostatic power loss and resist the accepted maximum drive force. Pump neutral alone is not a parking brake. Motor control options have different de-energized displacements, so choose a documented safe state instead of assuming every H1B behaves alike.

Operating stateFunctional requirement to developStill to define / prove
Start and stationary preparationVerify command neutral and actual pump/speed/brake state. Hold independent brakes during engine/charge warm-up. Interlock boarding doors/gates and emergency circuit before traction permission.Sensors, proof logic, warm-up/charge pressure criteria and release thresholds.
Depart and accelerateProve train brakes released using engineered means before applying tractive torque. Ramp torque/jerk and limit pressure/displacement by engine torque/power, pump shaft and adhesion constraints. Do not require full-speed engine for low rail speed by assumption.Controlled transition that avoids rollback, brake drag and torque surge. Exact engine-speed schedule and hydraulic map.
Normal runningMonitor independent vehicle speed, engine speed, both loop pressures, charge pressure, hottest oil temperature, case pressure where required and air readiness. Preserve a separate hotel-power load boundary.Fault thresholds, diagnostics, redundancy and component control code.
Service stopCoordinate traction removal and independent air-service braking. Hydrostatic retardation must be bounded against engine overspeed and low-loop pressure; it is not the sole stop or hold system.Blend timing, motor back-driving behavior, engine absorption and brake balance.
Reverse selectionRequire an accepted standstill criterion and brake hold, then neutral proof and a deliberate direction command. Reversing pump flow at speed is not a permitted shortcut.Exact forward/reverse mapping, switch interlocks and speed criteria.
Emergency, loss of command/power or pressureInhibit propulsion through an engineered safe path and initiate independent emergency braking appropriate to the fault. Verify spring-brake application cannot be inadvertently prevented or create uncontrolled wheel slide.Fault-tree review, energy reserves, fail states, application rate and complete consist response.
Recovery/towingSecure train mechanically and follow the delivered unit's restricted bypass/recovery instructions. Engine-off towing cannot assume charge flow or lubrication.Approved recovery speed/distance, isolation and redundant holding method.

The mean-speed conversion is useful for creep testing: 50 motor rpm corresponds to 0.390 mph with this wheel/reduction trial; 100 rpm corresponds to 0.781 mph. OEM low-speed guidance is not a smooth-boarding guarantee. Evaluate actual cogging, neutral creep, command resolution and safe low-speed operation with the accepted vehicle inertia and plumbing.

These are functional requirements for qualified control/brake engineering, not wiring, software, setpoints or approved operating instructions.

Procurement, prototype and crew responsibilities

First resolve exact engine/pump/motor configurations and duty. Then accept input coupling/adapter, reduction, axle drive, cooling, exhaust and brake interfaces before making production mounts. No availability, quote, OEM application acceptance or component order is claimed.

  1. Qualified engineers and suppliers review the exact order codes and instrumented bench plan.
  2. Test the guarded drive rig and actual component maps before a locomotive-only track test.
  3. Weigh the powered axles; check low-speed behavior, independent holding, reversing and fault response without passengers.
  4. Add ballast progressively under an accepted drawbar-force/brake plan.
  5. Perform thermal endurance with the complete shell, installed heat exchangers, compressor duty and aftertreatment events.

Farm crew: maintenance/access review, nonpowered clearance mockups and data collection under the approved plan. Specialists: final rail/drivetrain/brake/control engineering, critical machining/fabrication, clean hydraulic installation, engine emissions installation and commissioning. Exact acceptance criteria must be set by the responsible qualified team before physical tests.

Open sheet ↗

Loads from unequal braking

The model considers the locomotive, three regular coaches and the accessible coach in that order. It checks upper and tare-allocation mass cases, three trial brake-adhesion values, three trial traction values and 32 independent on/off brake patterns:576 train states, evaluated at four connections.

ConnectionLargest trial tensionLargest trial compression
J1 · Locomotive / C1110.5 kN55.3 kN
J2 · C1 / C2118.9 kN78.0 kN
J3 · C2 / C3101.0 kN74.6 kN
J4 · C3 / accessible56.8 kN44.8 kN

These values are sensitivity results, not design ratings. Brake-adhesion trials are 0.20 / 0.35 / 0.50, and traction trials are 0 / 0.30 / 0.50 times the full locomotive/hotel allocated weight. Actual powered weight, friction and controls are unresolved. The model uses 0% grade and excludes slack impact, draft-gear response, actual brake timing, curve forces, eccentricity, collision, snagging and derailment.

Force equations and part-load probe

Train acceleration a = (traction − total rail-brake force) / total mass. At each connection, the force on the tail is tail mass × a + tail brake force. Positive force means tension; negative means compression. Each binary brake state uses the trial rail force μmg, not an invented foundation-brake rating.

The DB-01part calculation separately applies 200 kN. At that probe, nominal gross axial stress is37.0 MPa, net axial stress at the 90 mm seat is92.6 MPa, and nominal projected bore pressure is61.7 MPa. These do not include local contact or notch stress. The ideal pin-ended Euler result is1799 kN; it is not a compression rating and needs inelastic/imperfection and joint checks.

The steel source lists minimum yield 50 ksi and tensile 65 ksi for this candidate plate range. That comparison alone cannot establish a fatigue-safe coupling. Eye contact analysis, fretting, out-of-plane loads, bearing seating and all mating joints remain necessary.

How this part would connect to the train

The proposed arrangement places a spherical plain bearing in each drawbar eye. A separately engineered pin/clevis connection anchors each eye into the coach underframe’s longitudinal load path. Pin-axis rotation permits yaw; spherical articulation accommodates the specified pitch/roll. The complete joint, surrounding clearances and draft gear must establish the usable movement.

Catalog comparison lead: SKF GE 60 ES-2RS is listed with 60 mm bore, 90 mm OD, 44 mm inner-ring width and 36 mm outer-ring width. Its catalog tilt is 6°, subject to shaft-shoulder and surrounding clearance. It is not an installed angular rating. Catalog dynamic/static figures 245 / 1,220 kN are spherical-plain-bearing data, not allowable rail-coupling loads. The rolling-bearing L10 method used in the earlier axle-bearing screen does not apply to this part.

The bar has no approved pin diameter/fit, bearing retainers, clevises, cushion/draft gear, frame bolts/welds, secondary restraint or guards. It must connect into engineered main-frame structure; end platforms, stair supports and body sheeting cannot be assumed to carry drawbar forces.

Hidden-under-platform appearance remains a packaging goal. Full turning and suspension travel, protected separation from passengers, pin removal, lubrication access, hoses and electrical jumpers must be checked before fixing the final length and height. This part does not create a passenger gangway.

Actual rail coupling alternatives

Dellner semi-permanent passenger couplers document a real fixed-consist arrangement with optional pneumatic/electrical interfaces and energy management. Irwin Willison 35990 and 3/4 Sharon are real industrial rail alternatives. Neither source supplies an accepted fit/rating for our train. A vehicle-tonnage category is not a tensile/compressive coupler-force rating. Custom fabrication remains the project’s primary development route.

Fabrication and inspection development sequence
  1. Accept train force/duty, car gap, curves and articulation requirements.
  2. Complete bearing arrangement, eye contact/fatigue, retainers, pins, clevises, draft gear and frame load path; revise the bar if needed.
  3. Obtain accepted certified plate, toughness and machining allowance.
  4. Define blank cutting, edge cleanup, machining datums, fits, finishes and tolerances.
  5. Issue material/first-article/NDT acceptance records and an engineer-approved guarded test plan.
  6. Review physical results before any repeat fabrication or passenger operation.

Farm crew: access review and non-load-bearing clearance mockups. Qualified specialists: final engineering, safety-critical machining/fabrication and inspection. No manufacturing or testing authorization is conveyed by this study.

3L.3-S3 · NEW PIECEWISE BODY / TRUCK SUPPORT LAW · 19 OCTOBER 2026

The wheels can stay loaded while the center bearing unloads.

Extends the previous linear roll-boundary study with explicit left/right sidebearer gaps and compression forces, center-bearing load sharing and the external body-roll reaction.13synthetic analytical fixtures exercise onset, left/right behavior, zero-gap comparison and center-contact loss.

No support hardware or mount dimensions are selected. This is an ideal gapped frictionless comparison with an imposed body angle—not the actual coach/other-truck roll solution or a model of a preloaded constant-contact product. The real center bearing, retained pivot, sidebearers and actual coil stations remain open.

Editable piecewise engineExact synthetic inputsEvery contact / reaction / branchPreserved four-corner model

How the support load is shared

For relative rollr=φ_truck−φ_body: right compressionδ_R=b_R·r−g_R, leftδ_L=−b_L·r−g_L. Each sidebearer carriesk·max(δ,0), never tension. Its moment on the truck isM_side=b_R·F_R−b_L·F_L.

The prescribed body downforce enters the truck equilibrium once. Sidebearers redistribute that load: R_center=W_body−F_R−F_L. Do not add sidebearer vertical forces on top of the original body force or subtract sprung truck-frame weight from centerR.

For each assumed contact branch, Kφ=Σk·b² and its equivalent reference includes the active gap terms. The engine enumerates all4branches, checks gap/moment consistency and deduplicates the same zero-force boundary solution. At exact onset, multiple branch labels do not mean multiple physical equilibria.

External reaction is explicit: the prescribed body needs an external roll moment−M_side to balance its upward side-contact couple. Other-truck/body torsion and the actual source of that reaction are not solved here. Positive force/moment balance is not free-body whole-coach stability.

Synthetic support checks—not proposed setup values

RetainS2base springs/contact geometry and30kN body reaction plus10kN sprung truck weight and8kN unsprung total. Sidebearer halfspan0.5m, rate900,000N/m and gaps5mm are deliberate test constants, not project mount positions or setup instructions. The center-unload diagnostic reduces only body reaction to2kN.

At railroll0.020/bodyroll0, the5mmgap example yields truckroll0.015, right side force2.25kN, center27.75kN and side moment1.125kNm. Reducing body reaction to2kN leaves positive wheel reactions but predicts center−0.25kN: the maintained-center-contact assumption fails. No lifted-center redistribution, retained-pivot tension capacity or safety permission is inferred.

Zero gap reproduces the prior linear-restraint result, but still has zero preload. It must not be substituted for a real constant-contact bearing’s preload, setup, friction or wear law. Pure diagonal warp remains unchanged when these symmetric positive gaps stay open; sidebearers cannot be presumed to cure track twist.

Decisions required before drawing the mounts

  • Support topology before hardware: Designer must choose and substantiate point/area center bearing, retained pivot and sidebearer law; gapped, preloaded and frictional systems cannot share unexamined mount heights/rates.
  • Center-bearing and retained pivot: Resolve compression support and any actual uplift/retention duty separately. A negative center diagnostic does not rate a pivot bolt or permit operation on it.
  • Actual spring stations: Obtain real upper/lower seat locations and guide/axlebox motion, including brake-reaction pitch. Two separated coils aggregate only with proven equal displacements; no actual8coil coordinates are closed.
  • Whole-coach and guide coupling: Body/other-truck roll/torsion, massCG, sidebearer preload/friction/contact/wear, yaw resistance and guide/brake reactions need the coordinated actual geometry/dynamics, not this imposed-body boundary.
  • Physical fabrication release: Actual contact plate areas/materials, spring/sidebearer curves, frame/crossmember/bolster/pivot joints, fits/bolts/welds/inspection require controlled interfaces and responsible engineering before dimensions are released.

Model limits

  • Isolated truck with externally prescribed body roll and prescribed vertical body reaction at an ideal center x=y0. Body heave follows maintained ideal center contact; whole body/other truck torsion and gravity roll are not solved.
  • The body support load enters vertical equilibrium ONCE. Sidebearers redistribute it: centerR=Wbody−FR−FL. Sprung truck-frame weight is separate and is not deducted from centerR again.
  • Frictionless, no-preload unilateral sidebearers at truck x0, with nonnegative gaps and linear compression rate. This is a comparison law, not the selected topology or actual product.
  • Zero gap here still has ZERO preload and is not a model of a real preloaded constant-contact bearing. No yaw/friction/damping/heat/setup/wear law or manufacturer equivalence is established.
  • Actual sidebearer/center contact stress, plate flexure, pivot shear/tension/retention, mount fatigue, yaw/pitch and wear clearances remain unmodeled.
  • When centerR is nonpositive (including numerical zero), maintained unilateral center contact is invalid. Raw negative/zero diagnostics remain; no lifted-center solution or pivot tension capacity is credited.
  • Sidebearer compression uses max(delta,0) as its DEFINED unilateral law. This is not clamping a negative wheel or center reaction. The latter invalidate their assumed contact regime.
  • The external body-roll moment needed to hold the imposed body angle is reported explicitly. It represents a required external reaction, not a designed or validated other-truck/body load path.
  • All3L.3-S2 spring-station, axle/contact, small-displacement, guide/friction/dynamic and no-safety-acceptance limitations remain. Actual paired-coil same-displacement assumptions must be established before aggregation.

Actual manufacturer context checked19October2026: Wabtec constant-contact family and Stucki side-bearing family. These establish real product categories only, not selection/compatibility/ratings for this36in train. A searched Stucki brochure link returned404; no cached setup/preload values were adopted.

3L.3-S2 · NEW FOUR-CORNER TRUCK EQUILIBRIUM · 19 OCTOBER 2026

Springs can stay compressed while a wheel unloads.

This fills a specific mechanics gap: priorS0covered uniform heave;S1covered axle stress, guide tilt and bearings. The new engine solves truck heave/pitch/roll, optional frame warp, each spring force and each axle’s two vertical wheel reactions. It does not create new mount geometry or complete the custom truck.

All displayed numerical fixtures are deliberately synthetic, not project hardware or permitted track defects. Actual wheel-contact/spring spacing, mass distribution, stiffness, sidebearer law and acceptance limits remain unselected.36in gauge is not substituted for a contact or spring span.

Editable equilibrium engineExact synthetic inputsEvery force / displacement / balanceDerivation / units / limitations

The rail-to-seat-to-wheel load path

  1. At each rigid equal-radius axle, railmean/crosslevel defines axle elevation. Interpolate/extrapolate to each actual lower spring-seat ycoordinate: u=(hR+hL)/2+y_s(hR−hL)/(2b_w).
  2. Solve upper truck-seat elevation z=h+xθ+yφ+s·w and compression force F=k(u−z), including explicit body reaction and sprung truck weight. Roll restraint is an imposed boundary, not a solved whole-coach response.
  3. Recover the two wheel reactions from each axle’s force AND moment balance: Q_R+Q_L=F_R+F_L+W_u; b_w(Q_R−Q_L)=b_s(F_R−F_L)+W_u·y_u+M_u.
  4. Check truck/global and each-axle force/moment residuals independently. Any nonpositive spring force or vertical wheel reaction puts the case outside assumed contact; retain the diagnostic, do not clamp it and pretend a wheel-lift solution.

b_s and b_w are spring and wheel-contact halfspans. If b_s/b_w=1.5 in a synthetic symmetric axle, diagonal spring-force change is amplified1.5times at the wheel. For a fixed rail-crosslevel difference, the rigid-frame wheel-load change includes a(b_s/b_w)²factor. This is why spring placement and axle leverage must be coordinated before mount forces are assigned.

Matrix and sign convention

Use normalized unknowns q=[h,aθ,b_sφ,w], all in metres. B_i=[1,x_i/a,y_i/b_s,s_i]; omitw for rigid frame. With C=diag(0,0,K_roll/b_s²,K_warp), solve(BᵀKB+C)q=BᵀKu−f+Cq_ref. Downward force moments enter f positively; pitch/roll are elevation slopes. The saved engine and derivation show units and complete balances.

Negative flatheave is a mathematical compression datum, not installed spring height or floor elevation. Physical free length/preload/seat geometry remains a separate unresolved design.

Independent analytical fixtures

Base fixture: aggregate corner rate100,000N/m, axle halfspacing1m, spring halfspan0.75m, rail-contact halfspan0.5m, body reaction30kN plus sprung truck weight10kN, and unsprung4kN per axle. These are validation constants, not a proposed36in truck. Baseline F=10kN/corner and Q=12kN/wheel.
